Acknowledgements

The Authors are grateful to Xinliang An for helpful discussions. Many
of our derivations were performed in xAct
[
30
]
for
Mathematica. This work was partially supported by FCT (Portugal) grant
UIDB/00099/2020. PZ was supported by the start-up fund of Beijing
Normal University at Zhuhai.

2
Geometric setting and formulation of the characteristic
initial value problem

In this section we provides details of the construction of Stewart’s
gauge and discuss the basic local existence result for the
characteristic initial value problem underlining our analysis. More
discussions can be found in
[
13
]
and
[
15
]

2.1
Coordinates and Stewart’s gauge

The basic geometric setting of this paper consists of a 4-dimensional
manifold
(\mathcal{M},{\bm{g}})
with boundary and an edge. The boundary
consists of two null hypersurfaces:
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
, the
outgoing null hypersurface; and
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
, the
incoming null hypersurface. We denote the non-empty intersection of
the initial hypersurfaces by

\mathcal{S}_{\star}\equiv\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
.
Given a neighbourhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
, the
future of
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
can be foliated by two families of
null hypersurfaces:
\mathcal{N}_{u}
(the outgoing null
hypersurfaces) and
\mathcal{N}_{v}^{\prime}
(the ingoing null
hypersurfaces). The functions
u
and
v
satisfy the eikonal equation
and
\mathcal{N}_{u_{\infty}}=\mathcal{N}_{\star}
,

\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
. Given suitable data on

(\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{U}
, one
can make statement of existence and uniqueness of solutions to the
Einstein-Scalar system on some open set

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\subset\{p\in\mathcal{U}|u(p)>u_{\infty},v(p)>0\}.

The relevant area for our analysis is given by the conditions

\displaystyle u_{\infty}\leq u\leq-a/4,\ \ 0\leq v\leq 1,

where
a
is a large positive number.

To complete the coordinate system, consider arbitrary coordinate
charts
(\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{\alpha},x_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{A}})

on
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
, with the index
A
taking the
values
2,\;3
and where
\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{\alpha}
denote a
finite cover of
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
. For convenience we can make use
of the stereographic coordinates given by the diffeomorphism of

\mathcal{S}_{\star}
with
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. In this case the chart label

\alpha
takes the values
1,\;2
corresponding, respectively, to the
North and South polar charts. Now, define

(\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{\alpha})_{u,0}
be the coordinate patch
generated from
\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{\alpha}
along the generators of

\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
, and
(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{u,v}
be the
coordinate patch generated from
(\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{\alpha})_{u,0}

along along the generators of each hypersurface
\mathcal{N}_{u}
. For
brevity we write
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}
to denote

(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{u,v}
. Coordinates
x_{\alpha}^{\mathcal{A}}

on
\mathcal{U}^{\star}_{\alpha}
are then first propagated into

\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
by requiring them to be constant along the
generators of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
. Once coordinates have been
defined on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
, one can propagate them into

\mathcal{V}
, a suitable neighbourhood of

\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
, by requiring them to be
constant along the generators of each
\mathcal{N}_{u}
. In this
manner one obtains a coordinate system

(x^{\mu})=(v,\ u,\ x^{\mathcal{A}})
in

D_{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}}\equiv\cup_{u,v}(\mathcal{U}_{\alpha})_{u,v}
.

The above coordinate construction leads in a natural way to a
Newman-Penrose (NP) null tetrad
\{{\bm{l}},{\bm{n}},\bm{m},\bar{\bm{m}}\}

with the vectors
{\bm{l}}
and
{\bm{n}}
tangent to the generators of the
null hypersurfaces
\mathcal{N}_{u}
and
\mathcal{N}_{v}^{\prime}

respectively, and
m^{a}
lying in the tangent space to the
intersection
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
. The auxiliary field

\varphi_{a}\equiv\frac{1}{3}\nabla_{a}\varphi
can be expanded in terms
of the NP frame and we write

\displaystyle\varphi_{0}

\displaystyle\equiv l^{a}\varphi_{a}=\frac{1}{3}l^{a}\nabla_{a}\varphi=\frac{1}{3}D\varphi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv n^{a}\varphi_{a}=\frac{1}{3}n^{a}\nabla_{a}\varphi=\frac{1}{3}\Delta\varphi,

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv m^{a}\varphi_{a}=\frac{1}{3}m^{a}\nabla_{a}\varphi=\frac{1}{3}\delta\varphi.

Following the same discussion of
[
13
]
we make the
following:

Gauge Choice 1

(
Stewart’s choice of the components of the
frame
)
.

On
\mathcal{V}
we consider a NP frame of the form

\displaystyle{\bm{l}}=Q{\bm{\partial}}_{v},\qquad{\bm{n}}={\bm{\partial}}_{u}+C^{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\partial}}_{\mathcal{A}},\qquad{\bm{m}}=P^{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\partial}}_{\mathcal{A}},

(4)

where
C^{\mathcal{A}}=0
on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
,
{\bm{m}}
and

\bar{\bm{m}}
span the tangent space
of
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
. On
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
one has that

{\bm{l}}=Q{\bm{\partial}}_{v}
. As the coordinates
(x^{\mathcal{A}})
are
constant along the generators of
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
and

\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
, it follows that on

\mathcal{N}_{\star}
the coefficient
Q
is only a function of

v
. Thus, without loss of generality one can parameterise
u
so as
to set
Q=1
on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
.

A direct application of the NP commutators to the
coordinates
(v,u,x^{2},x^{3})
leads to the following:

Lemma 1

(
conditions on the connection coefficients
)
.

The NP frame of Gauge Choice
1
can
be chosen such that

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=\nu=\epsilon=0,

(5a)

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=\bar{\rho},\ \ \mu=\bar{\mu},

(5b)

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\bar{\alpha}+\beta,

(5c)

on
\mathcal{V}
and, furthermore, with

\displaystyle\gamma-\bar{\gamma}=0\ \ \ on\ \ \ \mathcal{V}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}.

Remark 2
.

Additional commutator relations can be used to obtain equations
for frame coefficient
Q
,
P^{\mathcal{A}}

and
C^{\mathcal{A}}
. We have

\displaystyle DC^{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=(\bar{\tau}+\pi)P^{\mathcal{A}}+(\tau+\bar{\pi})\bar{P}^{\mathcal{A}},

(6a)

\displaystyle DP^{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\rho}P^{\mathcal{A}}+\sigma\bar{P}^{\mathcal{A}},

(6b)

\displaystyle\Delta P^{\mathcal{A}}-\delta C^{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=-(\mu-\gamma+\bar{\gamma})P^{\mathcal{A}}-\bar{\lambda}\bar{P}^{\mathcal{A}},

(6c)

\displaystyle\Delta Q

\displaystyle=(\gamma+\bar{\gamma})Q,

(6d)

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}P^{\mathcal{A}}-\delta\bar{P}^{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=(\alpha-\bar{\beta})P^{\mathcal{A}}-(\bar{\alpha}-\beta)\bar{P}^{\mathcal{A}},

(6e)

\displaystyle\delta Q

\displaystyle=(\tau-\bar{\pi})Q.

(6f)

,
[formula]
,
[formula]

Figure 2:
Setup for Stewart’s gauge. The construction makes use of a
double null foliation of the future domain of dependence of the
initial hypersurface
\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
. The
coordinates and NP null tetrad are adapted to this geometric
setting. See the main text for the definitions of
the various regions and objects.

2.1.1
The renormalised Weyl tensor and Bianchi identities

Once the NP frame has been specified one can obtain the structure
equations, the Bianchi Identities and the matter equations associated
to the Einstein-scalar field system. For the scalar field we
introduce an auxiliary field,
\varphi_{a}
corresponding to its
derivative. Then the scalar field equation can be recast as a
symmetric hyperbolic system for
\varphi
and
\varphi_{a}

—see (
54a
)-(
54e
) in
Appendix
A.2
. In fact, as it will
be seen in the main part of the article, these equations can be
formulated as a Hodge system (as defined for instance
in
[
8
]
).

Recall that from the Einstein field equations one has that the Ricci
curvature equals
\nabla_{a}\varphi\nabla_{b}\varphi
. To expand the
Bianchi Identity with respect to NP frame, we need derivatives of
curvature. It can the be seen that the equations for the Weyl
curvature components
\Psi_{1}
,
\Psi_{2}
and
\Psi_{3}
have the form

\displaystyle(D,\Delta)\bm{\Psi}-\delta\bm{\Psi}+\bm{\varphi}(D,\Delta)\bm{\varphi}-\bm{\varphi}\delta\bm{\varphi}=...

where
\bm{\varphi}
represents the auxiliary fields
\varphi_{0}
,

\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
. Equations of this form cannot be used to
formulate a Hodge system from which energy estimates can be
extracted. For this, one can only have one null derivative in the
equation. This problem can be solved by introducing the

renormalised quantities

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\Psi_{1}-\Phi_{01}=\Psi_{1}-3\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1},

(7a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\Psi_{2}+2\Lambda=\Psi_{2}-\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}+\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1},

(7b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv\Psi_{3}-\Phi_{21}=\Psi_{3}-3\varphi_{2}\bar{\varphi}_{1}.

(7c)

With these three renormalised Weyl curvature components, the terms

D\bm{\varphi}
and
\Delta\varphi
in the Bianchi identities are
absorbed in
D{\tilde{\Psi}}
,
\Delta{\tilde{\Psi}}
and
\delta{\tilde{\Psi}}
. Hence, it is
possible to formulate a Hodge system for the renormalised components
of the Weyl curvature. The resulting equations are
(
56a
)-(
56h
) in
Appendix
A.2
.

It is worth comparing the above construction with that for the
Einstein-Maxwell system, where the renormalisation
excludes
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}
. The reason for this difference can be traced back
to the fact that for the Einstein-Maxwell system one
has
\Lambda=R/24=0
. More details can be found in
[
28
]
.

2.2
The formulation of the characteristic initial value
problem for the Einstein-scalar system

In this section we discuss the general aspects of the characteristic
initial value problem (CIVP) for the Einstein-scalar (ES) system with
data on the null hypersurfaces
\mathcal{N}_{\star}

and
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
. The existence of a hierarchical structure
in the
reduced ES system
leads to a
reduced initial data
set
from which the full initial data on

\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
for the ES system can be
computed. In a situation where the initial data on

\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
is Minkowskian, due to the underlying
symmetries, there is more freedom on the choice of the reduced initial
data.

Lemma 2

(
freely specifiable data for the CIVP for the
ES system
)
.

Assume that the gauge conditions given by
Lemma
1
are satisfied in a
neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
. Initial data for
the ES system on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}
can be
computed from the reduced data set
\mathbf{r}_{\star}
consisting of:

\displaystyle\Psi_{0},\ \ \varphi_{0}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{N}_{\star},

\displaystyle\Psi_{4},\ \ \varphi_{2},\ \ \gamma+\bar{\gamma}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star},

\displaystyle\rho,\ \ \sigma,\ \ \mu,\ \ \lambda,\ \ \tau,\ \ \varphi_{1},\ \ P^{\mathcal{A}}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{S}_{\star}.

Proof.

Data on
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
.
From the frame coefficients

P^{\mathcal{A}}
one can compute the intrinsic 2-metric on

\mathcal{S}_{\star}
and the associated NP operators
\delta
,

\bar{\delta}
. Then from (
6f
) one has

\pi=\tau=\bar{\alpha}+\beta
. From the 2-metric one can compute

\beta-\bar{\alpha}
and hence we can obtain
\alpha

and
\beta
. From (
55n
),
(
55q
)
and (
55j
) we can compute the values
of
\Psi_{1}
,
\Psi_{2}
and
\Psi_{3}
.

Data on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
.
From
Q=1
we have

D=\partial_{v}
and
\tau=\bar{\pi}
. From the coefficients
\Psi_{0}
and

\varphi_{0}
we can solve (
55f
) and
(
55m
) together and obtain the value of

\rho
and
\sigma
. Then one can solve the equation for

P^{\mathcal{A}}
(
6b
) and obtain the operators

\delta
and
\bar{\delta}
along
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
. Combining
(
56b
), (
54c
) and
(
55b
) we can compute the values of

{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}
,
\varphi_{1}
and
\tau
. Next, we can solve
(
6a
) and obtain
C^{\mathcal{A}}
. From
(
55e
) and
(
55l
) we can obtain the value of
\beta

and
\alpha
. Solving (
55a
),
(
55h
),
(
55j
) and
(
55p
) together one can obtain the values
of
\gamma
,
\mu
,
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}
and
\lambda
. With the results above
in hand, from (
56f
),
(
56h
) and (
54d
) we
can obtain the coefficients
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}
,
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{4}
and
\varphi_{2}

on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
.

Data on
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}
.
The condition

C^{\mathcal{A}}=0
leads to
\Delta=\partial_{u}
. With the value
of
\gamma+\bar{\gamma}
on
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}
we can solve
(
6d
) and obtain
Q
. Solving
(
55g
) and
(
55o
) together we have
\mu

and
\lambda
. Next, solving (
6c
) and we
have
P^{\mathcal{A}}
which gives us the operators
\delta

and
\bar{\delta}

along
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
. Combining (
55d
),
(
55k
), (
54b
),
(
56g
), (
55c
)
and (
6e
) and solving as a system we can
determine
\beta
,
\alpha
,
\varphi_{1}
,
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}
,
\pi

and
\tau
. Solving (
55i
),
(
55r
) and
(
55j
) gives the values of
\sigma
,

\rho
and
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}
. With the results above we can obtain

\varphi_{0}
from (
54a
), obtain
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}

from (
56c
) and obtain
\Psi_{0}

from (
56a
).
∎

In the present analysis we are interested in a situation in which the
data for the ingoing lightcone is Minkowskian. In this case we have
another choice for the reduced initial data. Namely, one has:

Lemma 3

(
freely specifiable data of the
CIVP for ES system on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
)
.

Assume the gauge choice
1
and the gauge
conditions implied by Lemma
1
are satisfied in a
neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
. In addition
assume that the data on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
is Minkowskian.
Then the freely specifiable initial data for the ES system on

\mathcal{N}_{\star}
is given
\sigma
and
\varphi_{0}
.

Proof.

On
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
we have
Q=1
and this leads to

\tau=\bar{\pi}
and
D=\partial_{v}
. With the given data
\sigma
and

\varphi_{0}
, the value of
\rho
can be obtained using the structure
equation (
55m
). Then, the value of

\Psi_{0}
can be directly computed by
(
55f
) —this requires
D\sigma
. In
addition, with the value of
\rho
and
\sigma
, we can obtain

P^{\mathcal{A}}
via (
6b
) and, hence, the
operators
\delta
and
\bar{\delta}
. From
(
54c
) we can obtain the value of
\varphi_{1}

—
\delta\varphi_{0}
is needed for
this. Combining (
55b
),
(
55e
),
(
55l
)
and (
56b
) and solving together we can
obtain the values of
\tau
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}
—the
value of
\delta\Psi_{0}
is needed for
this. From (
55j
), with the value
of
\delta\alpha
and
\bar{\delta}\beta
we can compute
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}
.
Solving (
55h
)
and (
55p
) together we have
\mu

and
\lambda
—
\delta\pi
is needed for this. In addition, we can
obtain
\gamma
form (
55a
). Equation
(
54d
) leads to
\varphi_{2}

where
\bar{\delta}\varphi_{1}
is
needed. From (
55n
) we can
obtain
{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}
after
\delta\lambda
and
\bar{\delta}\mu
have
been obtained. With the information above at hand
\Psi_{4}
can be
obtained via (
56h
)
where
\bar{\delta}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}
is needed to do this.
∎

Remark 3
.

From the analysis in the previous proof it follows
that the regularity requirements on
\sigma
is are of least four
angular derivatives and one extra outgoing derivative. We need to
know at least two angular derivatives of
\varphi_{0}
.

Remark 4
.

The coefficient
\sigma
corresponds to the shear
of the outgoing lightcone. The coefficient
\varphi_{0}
describes
the rate of change of the scalar field along the
hypersurface
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
. This interpretation allows us
to understand the role of scalar field in the process of formation
of trapped surfaces. In this work the formation of trapped
surfaces follows from the interaction of gravity and matter.
When
\varphi_{0}
is zero, the pure gravity case, our result
collapses to that of
[
28
]
.

To apply the standard theory of the CIVP we extract a symmetric
hyperbolic system (SHS) from the ES system. Following the strategy
in
[
12
]
, in order to avoid the derivatives of
\varphi_{a}
in
the Bianchi Identity, to close the equations we introduce the
trace-free symmetric tensor
\hat{\varphi}_{ab}
and the
scalar
\hat{\varphi}

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{ab}\equiv\nabla_{a}\varphi_{b}-\frac{1}{4}\hat{\varphi}g_{ab},\qquad\hat{\varphi}\equiv\nabla_{c}\varphi^{c}.

It follows then that thee ES system is equivalent to the first order
system

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &[{\bm{e}}_{a},{\bm{e}}_{b}]-(\Gamma_{a\phantom{c}b}^{\phantom{a}c}-\Gamma_{b\phantom{c}a}^{\phantom{b}c}){\bm{e}}_{c}=0,\\
&R_{abc}^{\phantom{abc}d}-\rho_{abc}^{\phantom{abc}d}=0,\\
&\nabla_{[a}R_{bc]de}=0,\\
&\nabla_{a}\varphi-3\varphi_{a}=0,\\
&\nabla_{a}\varphi_{b}-\hat{\varphi}_{ab}-\frac{1}{4}\hat{\varphi}g_{ab}=0,\\
&\hat{\varphi}=0,\\
&\nabla_{a}\hat{\varphi}=0,\\
&\nabla_{[a}\hat{\varphi}_{b]c}-\frac{1}{2}R_{abc}^{\phantom{abc}d}\varphi_{d}=0,\\
&R_{ab}-6\varphi_{a}\varphi_{b}=0,\end{aligned}\right.

(8)

where
\rho_{abc}^{\phantom{abc}d}
is the
algebraic curvature

as defined by irreducible decomposition of the Riemann curvature in
terms of the Weyl and Ricci tensors. We denote the above first order
system as
\mathscr{N}=0
.

We now apply the same
strategy as in
[
13
]
and
[
15
]
and define

\displaystyle\bm{e}^{t}

\displaystyle\equiv\{C^{\mathcal{A}},P^{\mathcal{A}},Q\},

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}^{t}

\displaystyle\equiv\{\gamma,\pi,\beta,\mu,\alpha,\lambda,\tau,\sigma,\rho\},

\displaystyle\bm{\Psi}^{t}

\displaystyle\equiv\{\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4}\},

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}^{t}

\displaystyle\equiv\{\varphi_{0},\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\},

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\varphi}}^{t}

\displaystyle\equiv\{\hat{\varphi}_{00},\hat{\varphi}_{01},\hat{\varphi}_{02},\hat{\varphi}_{11},\hat{\varphi}_{12},\hat{\varphi}_{22}\},

where
\hat{\varphi}_{00}\equiv\hat{\varphi}_{ab}l^{a}l^{b}
,

\hat{\varphi}_{01}\equiv\hat{\varphi}_{ab}l^{a}m^{b}
,

\hat{\varphi}_{02}\equiv\hat{\varphi}_{ab}m^{a}m^{b}
,

\hat{\varphi}_{11}\equiv\hat{\varphi}_{ab}l^{a}n^{b}=\hat{\varphi}_{ab}m^{a}\bar{m}^{b}
,

\hat{\varphi}_{12}\equiv\hat{\varphi}_{ab}n^{a}m^{b}
,

\hat{\varphi}_{22}\equiv\hat{\varphi}_{ab}n^{a}n^{b}
. The naming here is
given to coincide with the components in spinorial language. By
selecting particular NP equations
for
\bm{u}^{t}=\{\varphi,\bm{e}^{t},\bm{\Gamma}^{t},\bm{\Psi}^{t},\bm{\varphi}^{t},\hat{\bm{\varphi}}^{t}\}

out of the first order system
\mathscr{N}=0
, one can extract the SHS

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{D}}\bm{u}=\bm{B}(\bm{e},\bm{\Gamma},\bm{\varphi},\hat{\bm{\varphi}},\bm{\Psi}),

(9)

where

\displaystyle\bm{\mathcal{D}}\bm{u}=\bm{A}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bm{u},

and
\bm{A}^{\mu}
is Hermitian and
\bm{A}^{\mu}(l_{\mu}+n_{\mu})
is positive.

To complete the analysis one needs to show that a solution to the SHS
(
9
) implies a solution to
\mathscr{N}=0
—that is, one
needs to prove the
propagation of the constraints
. The idea
behind this argument is that one can first define the constraints in

\mathscr{N}=0
as zero-quantities and then show that the latter
satisfies an homogeneous SHS under the assumption that (
9
)
holds. Although straightforward the explicit steps are technical and
lengthy, and so are not given explicitly. A similar example can be
found in
[
17
]
.

3
The T-weight formalism

In this section we introduce the
T-weight formalism
—an
extension of the GHP formalism used in our analysis.

3.1
From the NP to the T-weight formalism

As a part of the PDE analysis in the present article we routinely need
to integrate the norm of NP quantities globally over a topological
2-sphere
\mathcal{S}\approx\mathbb{S}^{2}
. However, strictly
speaking, NP coefficients are only defined on single coordinate
patches. Frames on the overlap region between coordinate patches are
related by rotation
m^{a}\mapsto e^{i\theta}m^{a}
. For a quantity which
transforms like
f\mapsto e^{is\theta}f
, namely with a definite
spin-weight, the norm
|f|^{2}=f\bar{f}
can be well defined all over
the 2-spheres. Therefore, the integral of
|f|^{2}
over
\mathcal{S}

makes sense. At the same time, such quantities are connected directly
to tensors over
\mathcal{S}
which depend only on the choice
of
\bm{l}
and
\bm{n}
and do not need a particular choice
of
\bm{m}
and
\bar{\bm{m}}
. Hence, for fixed
\bm{l}

and
\bm{n}
, one can define tensors on a topological
2-sphere
\mathcal{S}
associated to these quantities via the
relations

\displaystyle\sigma_{ab}\equiv\sigma\bar{m}_{a}\bar{m_{b}},\quad\lambda_{ab}\equiv\lambda m_{a}m_{b},\quad\tau_{a}\equiv\tau\bar{m}_{a},\quad\pi_{a}\equiv\pi m_{a},

(10a)

\displaystyle\kappa_{a}\equiv\kappa\bar{m}_{a},\quad\nu_{a}\equiv\nu m_{a},\quad\vartheta_{a}\equiv\vartheta\bar{m}_{a},

(10b)

\displaystyle(\Psi_{0})_{ab}\equiv\Psi_{0}\bar{m}_{a}\bar{m_{b}},\quad(\Psi_{1})_{a}\equiv\Psi_{1}\bar{m}_{a},\quad(\Psi_{3})_{a}\equiv\Psi_{3}m_{a},\quad(\Psi_{4})_{ab}\equiv\Psi_{4}m_{a}m_{b},

(10c)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{01})_{a}\equiv\Phi_{01}\bar{m}_{a},\quad(\Phi_{02})_{ab}\equiv\Phi_{02}\bar{m}_{a}\bar{m}_{b},\quad(\Phi_{12})_{a}\equiv\Phi_{12}\bar{m}_{a},

(10d)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{10})_{a}\equiv\Phi_{10}m_{a},\quad(\Phi_{20})_{ab}\equiv\Phi_{20}m_{a}m_{b},\quad(\Phi_{21})_{a}\equiv\Phi_{21}m_{a}.

(10e)

where
\vartheta\equiv\beta+\bar{\alpha}
. In addition, we consider the
following scalars with
0
spin-weight:

\displaystyle{\underline{\omega}}\equiv\gamma+\bar{\gamma}=l^{a}n^{b}\nabla_{b}n_{a},\quad\omega\equiv\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}=-n^{a}l^{b}\nabla_{b}l_{a},\quad\mu,\quad\rho,

\displaystyle\Psi_{2},\quad\Phi_{00},\quad\Phi_{11},\quad\Phi_{22},\quad\Lambda=R/24.

(11)

It is readily verified that the above tensors and scalars are
invariant under rotations
m^{a}\mapsto e^{i\theta}m^{a}
. Therefore, the
tensors and scalars above are defined globally on
\mathcal{S}
. It
also follows from the above definitions that the norms of the tensors
coincide with the norm of the associated NP quantities.

Taking into account the transformation laws for the above objects, we
define, for fixed
{\bm{l}}
and
{\bm{n}}
, a new weight
(
T-weight
)
s(f)
to the associated NP scalars:

Table 1:
T-weight of NP quantities

s=-2

\sigma
,
\Psi_{0}
,
\Phi_{02}

s=-1

\tau
,
\kappa
,
\vartheta
,
\Psi_{1}
,
\Phi_{01}
,
\Phi_{12}

s=0

\rho
,
\mu
,
{\underline{\omega}}
,
\omega
,
\Psi_{2}
,
\Phi_{00}
,
\Phi_{11}
,
\Phi_{22}
,
\Lambda

s=1

\pi
,
\nu
,
\Psi_{3}
,
\Phi_{10}
,
\Phi_{21}

s=2

\lambda
,
\Psi_{4}
,
\Phi_{20}

Under a rotation
m_{a}\mapsto e^{i\theta}m_{a}

a quantity
f
with T-weight
s
transforms as

\displaystyle f\mapsto e^{-is\theta}f.

Hence, for any T-weighted quantities
f
and
g
, we have

\displaystyle s(\bar{f})=-s(f),\qquad s(fg)=s(f)+s(g).

The coordinates
\overline{x}=(v,u,x^{\mathcal{A}})
are defined as
having
0
T-weight. For any quantity
f
in
Table
1
with T-weight
s(f)
, denote
T(f)
be
its corresponding tensor
in (
10a
)-(
10e
) and
(
11
). Up to this point the T-weight of an objects
coincides with its standard spin-weight up to a conventional minus
sign.
The main differences arise in the following discussions
about operators.

Remark 5
.

Combining the fields
in (
10a
)-(
10e
),
and (
11
) and their complex conjugates one can
construct the following real tensors:

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}_{ab}\equiv\sigma_{ab}+\bar{\sigma}_{ab}=-\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(\nabla_{\{b}l_{a\}}),\quad\underline{\hat{\chi}}_{ab}\equiv\lambda_{ab}+\bar{\lambda}_{ab}=\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(\nabla_{\{b}n_{a\}}),

(12a)

\displaystyle\eta_{a}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(\tau_{a}+\bar{\tau}_{a})=-\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(n^{b}\nabla_{b}l_{a}),\quad\underline{\eta}_{a}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(\pi_{a}+\bar{\pi}_{a})=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(l^{b}\nabla_{b}n_{a}),

(12b)

\displaystyle\zeta_{a}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(\beta+\bar{\alpha})\bar{m}_{a}+(\bar{\beta}+\alpha)m_{a}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(l^{b}\nabla_{a}n_{b}),

(12c)

\displaystyle\varkappa_{a}\equiv\kappa_{a}+\bar{\kappa}_{a}=-\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(l^{b}\nabla_{b}l_{a}),\quad\upsilon_{a}\equiv\nu_{a}+\bar{\nu}_{a}=\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(n^{b}\nabla_{b}n_{a}),

(12d)

and

\displaystyle\alpha_{ab}\equiv(\Psi_{0})_{ab}+(\bar{\Psi}_{0})_{ab}=\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(C_{lalb}),\quad\beta_{a}\equiv\frac{1}{2}[(\Psi_{1})_{a}+(\bar{\Psi}_{1})_{a}]=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(C_{lnla}),

(13a)

\displaystyle\underline{\beta}_{a}\equiv\frac{1}{2}[(\Psi_{3})_{a}+(\bar{\Psi}_{3})_{a}]=\frac{1}{2}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(C_{nlna}),\quad\underline{\alpha}_{ab}\equiv(\Psi_{4})_{ab}+(\bar{\Psi}_{4})_{ab}=\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(C_{nanb}).

(13b)

In the above expressions
\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the projection on
to
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
and indices
{\{ab\}}
denote the operation of
taking the traceless part. The notation on the left-hand-side of these
expressions is consistent with that of Christodoulou in
[
7
]
.

3.2
T-weight Operators and Norms

Next, we introduce differential operators which preserve the T-weight
as well as reflect the relation with tensor and covariant
derivative. A direct computation shows that the standard
operators
D
,
\Delta
,
\delta
and
\bar{\delta}
do not interact
simply with the T-weight.

The differential operators of the GHP formalism preserve the
spin-weight property but cannot be applied to quantities with
non-vanishing boost weight. To overcome this we overwrite the usual
GHP notation, and define four new differential operators
\mathchar 1264\relax
,

\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
,
\mathchar 1278\relax
and
\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax f

\displaystyle\equiv\delta f+s(\beta-\bar{\alpha})f,

(14a)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f

\displaystyle\equiv\bar{\delta}f-s(\bar{\beta}-\alpha)f,

(14b)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax f

\displaystyle\equiv Df+s(\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon})f,

(14c)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}f

\displaystyle\equiv\Delta f+s(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})f,

(14d)

acting on any quantity
f
with defined T-weight
s
. One can check
that under a rotation
m^{a}\mapsto e^{i\theta}m^{a}
, these operators
transform as

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax f\mapsto e^{-i(s-1)\theta}\mathchar 1264\relax f,\qquad\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f\mapsto e^{-i(s+1)\theta}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f,

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax f\mapsto e^{-is\theta}\mathchar 1278\relax f,\qquad\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}f\mapsto e^{-is\theta}\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}f.

Thus we set

\displaystyle s(\mathchar 1264\relax f)=s(f)-1,\quad s(\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f)=s(f)+1,\quad s(\mathchar 1278\relax f)=s(f)=s(\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}f).

(15)

Moreover, one can check that these four operators follow the Leibnitz
rule. Making use of
s(\bar{f})=-s(f)
, one has

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\bar{f}

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathchar 1264\relax f},\quad\mathchar 1264\relax\bar{f}=\overline{\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f},

(16)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathchar 1278\relax f}

\displaystyle=\mathchar 1278\relax\bar{f},\quad\overline{\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}f}=\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\bar{f}.

(17)

In our analysis we will also need to consider NP coefficients which do
not transform homogeneously under a rotation
f\mapsto e^{is\theta}f
. These include the combinations

\displaystyle\beta-\bar{\alpha},\qquad\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon},\qquad\gamma-\bar{\gamma}.

These quantities are related to the covariant derivative of the frame
on the 2-sphere along
{\bm{l}}
and
{\bm{n}}
. This connection can be seen
by introducing two new operators
\mathop{D}\limits_{\leftarrow}

and
\mathop{\Delta}\limits_{\leftarrow}
which are the projections
to
\mathcal{S}
of the covariant derivatives
l^{a}\nabla_{a}

and
n^{a}\nabla_{a}
. Denoting by
/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a}
the induced connection,
i.e. the covariant derivative on
\mathcal{S}
we have that

\displaystyle/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a}m_{b}

\displaystyle=(\beta-\bar{\alpha})\bar{m}_{a}m_{b}-(\bar{\beta}-\alpha)m_{a}m_{b},

\displaystyle\mathop{D}\limits_{\leftarrow}m_{a}

\displaystyle=(\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon})m_{a},\quad\mathop{\Delta}\limits_{\leftarrow}m_{a}=(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})m_{a}.

Now, given a T-weighted quantity
f
, denote its associated tensor
(cf. equations (
10a
)-(
10e
))
by
T(f)
. It follows from the preceding discussion that
\mathchar 1278\relax f

is the single non-vanishing component
of
\mathop{D}\limits_{\leftarrow}T(f)
;
\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}f
is the single
non-vanishing component of
\mathop{\Delta}\limits_{\leftarrow}T(f)
;

\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f
,
\mathchar 1264\relax^{k-1}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f
, …,
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime k}f
are the components
of
/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1}}.../\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{k}}T(f)
and so on. Taking the NP
scalar
\lambda
as an example one readily verifies
that
s(\lambda)=2
. Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1}}\lambda_{ab}

\displaystyle=[\bar{\delta}\lambda-2(\bar{\beta}-\alpha)\lambda]m_{a_{1}}m_{a}m_{b}+[\delta\lambda+2(\beta-\bar{\alpha})\lambda]\bar{m}_{a_{1}}m_{a}m_{b}

\displaystyle=(\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\lambda)m_{a_{1}}m_{a}m_{b}+(\mathchar 1264\relax\lambda)\bar{m}_{a_{1}}m_{a}m_{b},

\displaystyle\mathop{D}\limits_{\leftarrow}\lambda_{ab}

\displaystyle=[D\lambda+2(\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon})\lambda]m_{a}m_{b}=(\mathchar 1278\relax\lambda)m_{a}m_{b},

\displaystyle\mathop{\Delta}\limits_{\leftarrow}\lambda_{ab}

\displaystyle=[\Delta\lambda+2(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})\lambda]m_{a}m_{b}=(\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\lambda)m_{a}m_{b}.

One can also verify the transformation rules

\displaystyle\delta\lambda+2(\beta-\bar{\alpha})\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{-i\theta}[\delta\lambda+2(\beta-\bar{\alpha})\lambda],

\displaystyle\bar{\delta}\lambda-2(\bar{\beta}-\alpha)\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{-3i\theta}[\bar{\delta}\lambda-2(\bar{\beta}-\alpha)\lambda],

\displaystyle D\lambda+2(\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon})\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{-2i\theta}[D\lambda+2(\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon})\lambda],

\displaystyle\Delta\lambda+2(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})\lambda

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{-2i\theta}[\Delta\lambda+2(\gamma-\bar{\gamma})\lambda].

One also has that

\displaystyle|/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1}}\lambda_{ab}|^{2}=|\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\lambda|^{2}+|\mathchar 1264\relax\lambda|^{2}.

Similarly, one can compute
/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{2}}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1}}\lambda_{ab}

and has

\displaystyle|/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{2}}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1}}\lambda_{ab}|^{2}=|\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime 2}\lambda|^{2}+|\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\lambda|^{2}+|\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax\lambda|^{2}+|\mathchar 1264\relax^{2}\lambda|^{2}.

Hence, the norm of
/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)
can be computed in terms of the
norm of all its components
...\mathchar 1264\relax...\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}...f
, i.e. we have

\displaystyle|/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)|^{2}=\sum_{\alpha}|\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f|^{2},

where
\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f
is a string of order
k
of the
operators
\mathchar 1264\relax
and
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
, and the sum over
\alpha
denotes all
such strings. The set of all possible strings
\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}
is
contains all the possible combinations of
\mathchar 1264\relax
and
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
of
order
k
. As
\mathchar 1264\relax
and
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
do not, in general, commute, the
order of
\mathchar 1264\relax
and
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
in the string matters. For example
when
k=3
we have the combinations

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax^{3}f,\quad\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax^{2}f,\quad\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax f,\quad\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime 2}\mathchar 1264\relax f,\quad\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f,\quad\mathchar 1264\relax^{2}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f,\quad\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime 2}f,\quad\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime 3}f.

To obtain all the fourth order combinations one just needs to
apply
\mathchar 1264\relax
and
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
to each of the terms above. We see that, in
general, there are
2^{k}
terms in the collection of strings
in
\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f
.
Proceeding in this
way leads to the norm on
\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle||/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}=\sum_{\alpha}\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f|^{2}.

Now we can introduce the norms of T-weighted quantities —namely, we
define

\displaystyle||\mathcal{D}^{k}f||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathcal{D}^{k}f|^{2},

(18)

where

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}^{k}f|^{2}\equiv\sum_{\alpha}|\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f|^{2}.

(19)

We use the notation
\mathchar 1278\relax\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f

and
\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f
to denote the
\mathchar 1278\relax

and
\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}
-derivatives of
\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f

respectively. Making use of the commutator
relations (
60a
) and
(
60c
), one readily finds that the structure
of the equations for the various
quantities
\mathchar 1278\relax\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f
is similar. The only
differences arise in the terms involving
\mathchar 1264\relax
,
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
and the
conjugates of T-weighted quantities. These differences disappear once
the norm and the sum in (
18
) are evaluated —this
is an important observation in the analysis of the transport equations
and the construction of energy estimates. A similar discussion applies
to
\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f
. The differences between
\mathchar 1264\relax

and
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
will be addressed in the construction of elliptic
estimates.

Similarly to the above we define norms

\displaystyle||\mathcal{D}^{k}f||^{p}_{L^{p}(\mathcal{S})}\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathcal{D}^{k}f|^{p},

(20)

and

\displaystyle||\mathcal{D}^{k}f||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S})}\equiv\sup_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathcal{D}^{k}f|.

(21)

Remark 6
.

In the previous discussion, the notation
T(f)
has been used to
denote the tensor associated to
f
according to Table

1
. When we apply the derivatives
\mathchar 1264\relax
or
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}

to
f
, its associated tensor is defined by

\displaystyle...\mathchar 1264\relax...\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}...f\mapsto(...\mathchar 1264\relax...\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}...f)...\bar{m}_{a}...m_{b}...

In the following discussions, we will only consider quantities
f
and
associated tensors
T(f)
appearing in Table
1

and in equations (
10a
)-(
10e
)
and (
11
). From the definition
in (
19
), for any two quantities
f
and
g
,
the norm of the tensor product
/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k^{\prime}}T(g)

can be naturally defined as follows:

\displaystyle|/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k^{\prime}}T(g)|^{2}=(\sum_{\alpha}|\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f|^{2})(\sum_{\beta}|\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}_{i}}g|^{2})=|\mathcal{D}^{k}f|^{2}|\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}}g|^{2}.

Integral on
\mathcal{S}
we have

\displaystyle||\mathcal{D}^{k}f\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}}g||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}

\displaystyle\equiv||/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k^{\prime}}T(g)||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}(\sum_{\alpha}|\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f|^{2})(\sum_{\beta}|\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}_{i}}g|^{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathcal{D}^{k}f|^{2}|\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}}g|^{2}.

Hence for any product
\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}_{j}}g
we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}_{j}}g|\leq|\mathcal{D}^{k}f||\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}}g|,

which leads to

\displaystyle||\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}f\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}_{j}}g||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}\leq||\mathcal{D}^{k}f\mathcal{D}^{k^{\prime}}g||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}.

This follows from the definition given in (
20
). For
expressions involving the product of more than two terms we have
similar results. These observations supplement the previous
discussions on the relation between the norms for terms
involving
\mathcal{D}^{k_{i}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{k}
.

Remark 7

(Relation to the standard GHP formalism)
.

The connection coefficients,
\kappa
,
\rho
,
\sigma
,

\tau
,
\nu
,
\mu
,
\lambda
and
\pi
have GHP
weights
\{3,1\}
,
\{1,1\}
,
\{3,-1\}
,
\{1,-1\}
,
\{-3,-1\}
,

\{-1,-1\}
,
\{-3,1\}
and
\{-1,1\}
respectively. In addition
to these coefficients which also are considered in the GHP
formalism, we consider further three coefficients with definite
T-weight —namely,
{\underline{\omega}}=\gamma+\bar{\gamma}
,

\omega=\epsilon+\bar{\epsilon}

and
\vartheta=\beta+\bar{\alpha}
. Now, the Weyl curvature
coefficients
\Psi_{0}
,
\Psi_{1}
,
\Psi_{2}
,
\Psi_{3}
and
\Psi_{4}

have weight
\{4,0\}
,
\{2,0\}
,
\{0,0\}
,
\{-2,0\}

and
\{-4,0\}
respectively. The Ricci curvature
coefficients
\Phi_{00}
,
\Phi_{10}
,
\Phi_{01}
,
\Phi_{11}
,

\Phi_{20}
,
\Phi_{21}
,
\Phi_{02}
,
\Phi_{12}
and
\Phi_{22}

have weight
\{2,2\}
,
\{0,2\}
,
\{2,0\}
,
\{0,0\}
,

\{-2,2\}
,
\{-2,0\}
,
\{2,-2\}
,
\{0,-2\}
and
\{-2,-2\}

respectively. For any quantity
f
with GHP weight
\{w,z\}
we
have that

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax_{G}f

\displaystyle=Df+(-w\epsilon-z\bar{\epsilon})f,\qquad\mathchar 1278\relax_{G}^{\prime}f=\Delta f+(-w\gamma-z\bar{\gamma})f,

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax_{G}f

\displaystyle=\delta f+(-w\beta-z\bar{\alpha})f,\qquad\mathchar 1264\relax_{G}^{\prime}f=\bar{\delta}f+(-w\alpha-z\bar{\beta})f,

where we have used the subscript
G
to denote the GHP
operators. Similarly, we use the subscript
T
to denote the T-weight
operators. Then, for quantity
f
we have that

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax_{T}f-\mathchar 1264\relax_{G}f

\displaystyle=[(w+s)\beta+(z-s)\bar{\alpha}]f,\qquad\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}_{T}f-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}_{G}f=[(z-s)\bar{\beta}+(w+s)\alpha]f,

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax_{T}f-\mathchar 1278\relax_{G}f

\displaystyle=[(w+s)\epsilon+(z-s)\bar{\epsilon}]f\qquad\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}_{T}f-\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}_{G}f=[(w+s)\gamma+(z-s)\bar{\gamma}]f.

One can check that for quantities with zero boost-weight, the GHP
operators coincide with the T-weight operators. The difference comes
arises for non-zero boost-weighted quantities. To translate between
the formalism we can use the maps
w\mapsto s=\frac{w-z}{2}

and
z\mapsto-s=-\frac{w-z}{2}
.

The NP equations in the T-weight formalism are given in
Appendix
B
.

We conclude this section by noting the following lemma:

Lemma 4
.

Suppose
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are two real scalar fields with zero
T-weight. Then

\displaystyle||\mathcal{D}^{k}(f_{1}+if_{2})||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}=||\mathcal{D}^{k}f_{1}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}+||\mathcal{D}^{k}f_{2}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}.

Proof.

The proof follows from a direct computation. Namely, one has that

\displaystyle||\mathcal{D}^{k}(f_{1}+if_{2})||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}

\displaystyle=||/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}(f_{1}+if_{2})||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}[/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{a_{1},...,a_{k}}(f_{1}+if_{2})][/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1},...,a_{k}}(f_{1}-if_{2})]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{a_{1},...,a_{k}}f_{1}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1},...,a_{k}}f_{1}+\int_{\mathcal{S}}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{a_{1},...,a_{k}}f_{2}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a_{1},...,a_{k}}f_{2}

\displaystyle=||\mathcal{D}^{k}f_{1}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}+||\mathcal{D}^{k}f_{2}||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}.

∎

Remark 8
.

The above discussion shows that the
terms
\beta-\bar{\alpha}
,
\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon}

and
\gamma-\bar{\gamma}
allow us to connect the derivative of NP
quantities
f
with T-weight and the derivative of
tensors
T(f)
. In what follows we will not distinguish
f

and
T(f)
where there is no source for confusion.

3.3
The T-weight formalism and PDE analysis

In this section we discuss applications of the T-weight formalism to
the construction of estimates to solutions to differential equations
in a number of scenarios.

3.3.1
Transport estimates

Given a quantity
f
such that
s(f)=s
we have that

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax(f\bar{f})=D(f\bar{f})=f\mathchar 1278\relax\bar{f}+\bar{f}\mathchar 1278\relax f,\qquad\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}(f\bar{f})=\Delta(f\bar{f})=f\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\bar{f}+\bar{f}\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}f.

It follows that in order to obtain
L^{2}
-estimates of
/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{k}T(f)

one can apply the transport identities

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}\phi=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}Q^{-1}\left(D\phi-(\rho+\bar{\rho})\phi\right),

(22)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}\phi=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}\left(\Delta\phi+(\mu+\bar{\mu})\phi\right)

(23)

to
\phi\equiv f\bar{f}
, where we have made use of the expressions
for
{\bm{l}}
and
{\bm{n}}
in Stewart’s gauge —namely

\displaystyle{\bm{l}}=Q{\bm{\partial}}_{v},\qquad{\bm{n}}={\bm{\partial}}_{u}+C^{\mathcal{A}}{\bm{\partial}}_{\mathcal{A}}.

As an example of the above discussion consider
\phi=|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}
. In
this case the derivatives
\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f
and
\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f

encode the typical structural property
of
\mathop{D}\limits_{\leftarrow}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)
and

\mathop{\Delta}\limits_{\leftarrow}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{k}T(f)
. It follows then that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}Q^{-1}\left(D|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}-(\rho+\bar{\rho})|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}\right).

The left-hand side can be expanded as

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}=2||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

while the right hand side can be estimated as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}Q^{-1}\left(\mathchar 1278\relax|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}-(\rho+\bar{\rho})|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}Q^{-1}\left(\overline{\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f}\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f+\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f\mathchar 1278\relax\overline{\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f}-(\rho+\bar{\rho})|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}}2Q^{-1}\left(|\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|\dot{|}\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|+|\rho||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\left(||Q^{-1}\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||\rho||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||Q^{-1}\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\right).

Combining the above expressions one concludes that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}v}||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\leq\left(||Q^{-1}\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||\rho||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||Q^{-1}\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\right).

Similarly, one can show the inequality

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\leq\left(||\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||\mu||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||\mathchar 1264\relax^{k}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\right).

3.3.2
Elliptic estimates

We are particularly interested in constructing estimates for the
divergence and curl operators on the 2-spheres
\mathcal{S}_{u,v}
.
We note that the volume element
\varepsilon_{ab}
can be written
as
\varepsilon_{ab}=i(\bar{m}_{a}m_{b}-m_{a}\bar{m}_{b})
. For any NP
quantity
f
related tensor
T(f)
in Table
1
, we
define

\displaystyle(\mathrm{div}T(f))_{a_{1}...a_{r}}\equiv/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{a}T(f)_{aa_{1}...a_{r}},\qquad(\mathrm{curl}T(f))_{a_{1}...a_{r}}\equiv\varepsilon^{ab}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a}T(f)_{ba_{1}...a_{r}},

where
r=0,1
. One can readily verify that

\displaystyle\mathrm{curl}T(f)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}(\mathrm{div}T(f))=\mathrm{i}T(\mathchar 1264\relax f),\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{for}\quad s(f)>0,

\displaystyle\mathrm{curl}T(f)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}(\mathrm{div}T(f))=-\mathrm{i}T(\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f),\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{for}\quad s(f)<0.

It follows that the curl of the tensor
T(f)
is essentially encoded
in its divergence. For quantities in Table
1
with
nonzero T-weight, we will use the T-weighted scalar
\mathscr{D}_{f}
to
denote its divergence:

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{f}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathchar 1264\relax f&\quad s(f)>0,\\
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f&\quad s(f)<0.\end{aligned}\right.

(24)

When
f
has
s=0
, we have that

\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}f=(\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}+\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax)f,

where
\bm{\Delta}
is the Laplacian
/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{a}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla_{a}
. Now, for
a double null foliation we have
\mu=\bar{\mu}

and
\rho=\bar{\rho}
. This leads to

\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}f=2\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax f=2\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f.

3.3.3
Stokes theorem

As a topological 2-sphere
\mathcal{S}
has no boundary, it follows
then from Stokes theorem that for any 1-form
h_{a}
on
\mathcal{S}
,
the integral of a divergence vanishes —that is

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}/\kern-5.80002pt\nabla^{a}h_{a}=0.

Now, suppose we have two T-weighted quantities
f
and
g
satisfying

s(f)+s(g)-1=0
and define
h\equiv fg
. One can verify that

s(h)=1
. It follows then that the associated tensor is of the form

\displaystyle h_{a}=hm_{a}

which is globally defined on
\mathcal{S}
. One can check that

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}\bm{h}=\mathchar 1264\relax h=\mathchar 1264\relax(fg)=f\mathchar 1264\relax g+g\mathchar 1264\relax f,\qquad s(\mathrm{div}h)=0,

from which it follows that
f\mathchar 1264\relax g+g\mathchar 1264\relax f
is a globally defined
scalar on
\mathcal{S}
. Thus, applying Stokes Theorem one has

\displaystyle 0=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathrm{div}h=\int_{\mathcal{S}}f\mathchar 1264\relax g+g\mathchar 1264\relax f\Rightarrow\int_{\mathcal{S}}f\mathchar 1264\relax g=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}g\mathchar 1264\relax f.

Similarly, when
f
and
g
satisfy

\displaystyle s(f)+s(g)+1=0,

we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}f\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}g=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}g\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f.

We summarise the above discussion in the following:

Lemma 5
.

(i)

For a T-weighted quantity
h
with
s(h)=1
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 1264\relax h=0.

If, on the other hand
s(h)=-1
, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}h=0.

(ii)

For T-weighted quantities
f
and
g
on
\mathcal{S}
satisfying

\displaystyle s(f)+s(g)-1=0,

we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}f\mathchar 1264\relax g=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}g\mathchar 1264\relax f.

If, on the other hand, we have that

\displaystyle s(f)+s(g)+1=0,

then we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}f\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}g=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}g\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f.

The above lemma can be used to deal with a system of the form

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\Psi_{I}-\mathchar 1264\relax\Psi_{II}&=P,\\
\mathchar 1278\relax\Psi_{II}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\Psi_{I}&=Q,\end{aligned}\right.

where the T-weights of
\Psi_{I}
and
\Psi_{II}
satisfy

\displaystyle s(\Psi_{I})+s(\bar{\Psi}_{II})+1=0,\qquad s(\Psi_{II})+s(\bar{\Psi}_{I})-1=0.

From Lemma
5
it follows then that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}\Psi_{I}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\bar{\Psi}_{II}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}\bar{\Psi}_{II}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\Psi_{I},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}\Psi_{II}\mathchar 1264\relax\bar{\Psi}_{I}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}\bar{\Psi}_{I}\mathchar 1264\relax\Psi_{II}.

Therefore, the above system leads to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}\Delta(|\Psi_{I}|^{2})+\int_{\mathcal{S}}D(|\Psi_{II}|^{2})=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\Psi_{I}\bar{P}+\bar{\Psi}_{I}P+\Psi_{II}\bar{Q}+\bar{\Psi}_{II}Q\right).

Recall now that on a double null foliation one has that

\bar{\rho}=\rho
and
\bar{\mu}=\mu
. Hence, for a T-weight quantity
f

with T-weight
s
the commutation relation (
59f
)
simplifies to

\displaystyle(\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax)f=sKf,

with
K
the Gaussian curvature. It follows then that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathchar 1264\relax f|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\overline{\mathchar 1264\relax f}\mathchar 1264\relax f=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\bar{f}\mathchar 1264\relax f=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}\bar{f}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax f=-\int_{\mathcal{S}}\bar{f}\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f+\int_{\mathcal{S}}sK|f|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathchar 1264\relax\bar{f}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f+\int_{\mathcal{S}}sK|f|^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f|^{2}+\int_{\mathcal{S}}sK|f|^{2}.

In the particular case of a scalar
f
zero T-weight, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathchar 1264\relax f|^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f|^{2}.

3.3.4
Sobolev embeddings

In this subsection we briefly recall results from
[
7
]
,
Chapter 5.2, but adapted here to the T-weight formalism. Given any
T-weighted quantity
f
, we have that

\displaystyle\left(\mbox{Area}(\mathcal{S})\right)^{-1/p}||f||_{L^{p}(\mathcal{S})}\leq C_{p}\sqrt{I^{\prime}(\mathcal{S})}\left(\left(\mbox{Area}(\mathcal{S})\right)^{-1/2}||f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}+||\mathcal{D}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}\right),

(25)

for
2<p<\infty
, and

\displaystyle||f||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S})}\leq C_{p}\sqrt{I^{\prime}(\mathcal{S})}\left(\mbox{Area}(\mathcal{S})\right)^{1/2-1/p}\left(||f||_{L^{p}(\mathcal{S})}+\left(\mbox{Area}(\mathcal{S})\right)^{-1/2}||\mathcal{D}f||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S})}\right),

(26)

for
p>2
, where
C_{p}
is a numerical constant depending only on
p

and

\displaystyle I^{\prime}(S)\equiv\max\{I(\mathcal{S}),1\},

where
I(\mathcal{S})
is the isoperimetric constant of
\mathcal{S}
.

3.4
Local existence results in terms of the T-weight
formalism

In this section we make use of the T-weight formalism to reformulate
the local existence results for the characteristic initial value
problem given in
[
13
]
. This reformulation can be
directly obtained by a replacement of the spin connection coefficients
and differential operators by their T-weighted
counterpart. Accordingly, we only provide the main statements of the
theorems.

The article
[
13
]
is concerned with the local existence
of CIVP for the vacuum Einstein field equations (EFE). The analysis is
done in a coordinate system and gauge choice which are slightly
different from those considered in the previous section. In this case
the statement for the freely specifiable data reads as follows:

Lemma 6

(
Freely specifiable data for the CIVP
)
.

Working in the gauge discussed in
Section 2 in
[
13
]
, initial data for the vacuum
Einstein field equations
on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime}
can be
computed (near
\mathcal{S}_{\star}
) from a reduced data
set
\mathbf{r}_{\star}
consisting of:

\displaystyle\Psi_{0},\;\omega\quad\mbox{{on}}\quad\mathcal{N}_{\star},

\displaystyle\Psi_{4}\ \ \mbox{{on}}\ \ \mathcal{N}_{\star}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\lambda,\ \ \sigma,\ \ \mu,\ \ \rho,\ \ \pi,\ \ P^{\mathcal{A}}\ \ \mbox{{on}}\ \ \mathcal{S}_{\star}.

The existence result can be reformulated as:

Theorem 3

(
Improved local existence for the CIVP for
the EFE
)
.

Given regular initial data for the vacuum Einstein field equations as
constructed in Lemma
6
on the null
hypersurfaces
\mathcal{N}_{\star}\cup\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}

for
I\equiv\{0\leq v\leq v_{\bullet}\}
, there exists
\varepsilon>0

such that a unique smooth solution to the vacuum Einstein field
equations exists in the region where
v\in I

and
0\leq u\leq\varepsilon
defined by the null
coordinates
(u,v)
. The number
\varepsilon
can be chosen to depend
only on
I
, and the initial data

\displaystyle\Delta_{e_{\star}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sup_{\mathcal{N}_{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}}\sup_{U_{\alpha}}\left(|Q|,|Q^{-1}|,|C^{\mathcal{A}}|,|P^{\mathcal{A}}|\right),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Gamma_{\star}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sup_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}}\sup_{\Gamma\in\{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\sigma,\pi,\tau,\omega,\chi\}}\max\{1,||\Gamma||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},\sum_{i=0}^{1}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Gamma||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},\sum_{i=0}^{2}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Gamma||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\},

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Psi_{\star}}

\displaystyle\equiv\sup_{\mathcal{S}_{u,v}\subset\mathcal{N}_{\star},\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}}\sup_{\Psi\in\{\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4}\}}\max\{1,\sum_{i=0}^{1}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Psi||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},\sum_{i=0}^{2}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Psi||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\sup_{\Psi\in\{\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3}\}}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Psi||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{N}_{\star})}+\sup_{\Psi\in\{\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4}\}}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Psi||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star})}.

Furthermore, in this existence domain one has that

\displaystyle\sup_{u,v}\sup_{\Gamma\in\{\mu,\lambda,\rho,\sigma,\omega,\chi,\tau,\pi\}}\max\bigg\{\sum_{i=0}^{1}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Gamma||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},\sum_{i=0}^{2}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Gamma||_{L^{4}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},\sum_{i=0}^{3}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Gamma||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\bigg\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=0}^{3}\sup_{\Psi\in\{\Psi_{0},\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3}\}}\sup_{u}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Psi||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{N}_{u})}+\sup_{\Psi\in\{\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2},\Psi_{3},\Psi_{4}\}}\sup_{v}||\mathcal{D}^{i}\Psi||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v})}\leq C(I,\Delta_{e_{\star}},\Delta_{\Gamma_{\star}},\Delta_{\Psi_{\star}}).

Remark 9
.

These three quantities
\beta-\bar{\alpha}
,
\epsilon-\bar{\epsilon}
,

\gamma-\bar{\gamma}
depend on the choice of frame on
\mathcal{S}

and thus are not T-weight quantities. For these three we need to
choose an appropriate coordinate system to make sure that their
initial values do not blow up. Then we can solve the ODE
systems ((
55a
),
(
55d
)
and (
55k
)) for these three quantities
in the target area.

3.5
The Einstein-massless scalar field system in the T-weight
formalism

The use of Stewart’s gauge as discussed in
Section
2.1
together with the T-weight
formalism gives rise to a simpler version of the Einstein-scalar field
equations.

As already discussed, in Stewart’s gauge one has that

\displaystyle\kappa=0,\quad\nu=0,\quad\epsilon=0,

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}=\rho,\quad\bar{\mu}=\mu,\quad\vartheta=\tau.

It follows then that the equations for the scalar field take the form

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\varphi_{0}-\mathchar 1264\relax\bar{\varphi}_{1}

\displaystyle=({\underline{\omega}}-\mu)\varphi_{0}+\rho\varphi_{2}-\bar{\tau}\varphi_{1}-\tau\bar{\varphi}_{1},

(27a)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\bar{\varphi}_{1}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\varphi_{0}

\displaystyle=(\pi-\bar{\tau})\varphi_{0}+\bar{\sigma}\varphi_{1}+\rho\bar{\varphi}_{1},

(27b)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\varphi_{1}-\mathchar 1264\relax\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle=-\mu\varphi_{1}-\bar{\lambda}\bar{\varphi}_{1},

(27c)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\varphi_{2}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle=-\mu\varphi_{0}+\rho\varphi_{2}+\pi\varphi_{1}+\bar{\pi}\bar{\varphi}_{1},

(27d)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax\bar{\varphi}_{1}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle=0.

(27e)

Taking into account the definition of renormalised components of the
Weyl curvature as given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}_{1}\equiv\Psi_{1}-3\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1},\quad\tilde{\Psi}_{2}\equiv\Psi_{2}-\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}+\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1},\quad\tilde{\Psi}_{3}\equiv\Psi_{3}-3\varphi_{2}\bar{\varphi}_{1},

it follows that the structure equations are

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\rho

\displaystyle=\rho^{2}+\sigma\bar{\sigma}+3\varphi_{0}^{2},

(28a)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\sigma

\displaystyle=2\rho\sigma+\Psi_{0},

(28b)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\mu-\mathchar 1264\relax\pi

\displaystyle=\rho\mu+\sigma\lambda+\pi\bar{\pi}+{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2},

(28c)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\lambda-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\pi

\displaystyle=\rho\lambda+\bar{\sigma}\mu+\pi^{2}+3\bar{\varphi}_{1}^{2},

(28d)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\rho-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\tau

\displaystyle=-\rho\mu-\sigma\lambda-\bar{\tau}\tau+{\underline{\omega}}\rho-{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2},

(28e)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\sigma-\mathchar 1264\relax\tau

\displaystyle=-\mu\sigma-\bar{\lambda}\rho-\tau^{2}+{\underline{\omega}}\sigma-3\varphi_{1}^{2},

(28f)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mu

\displaystyle=-\mu^{2}-\lambda\bar{\lambda}-{\underline{\omega}}\mu-3\varphi_{2}^{2},

(28g)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\lambda

\displaystyle=-2\mu\lambda-{\underline{\omega}}\lambda-\Psi_{4},

(28h)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\tau

\displaystyle=(\tau+\bar{\pi})\rho+(\bar{\tau}+\pi)\sigma+{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}+6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1},

(28i)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\pi

\displaystyle=-(\pi+\bar{\tau})\mu-(\bar{\pi}+\tau)\lambda-{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}-6\varphi_{2}\bar{\varphi}_{1},

(28j)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax{\underline{\omega}}

\displaystyle=2\tau\bar{\tau}+2\tau\pi+2\bar{\tau}\bar{\pi}+{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+\bar{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2},

(28k)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\tau-\mathchar 1264\relax{\underline{\omega}}

\displaystyle=-2\bar{\tau}\bar{\lambda}-2\tau\mu-\bar{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}-6\varphi_{2}\varphi_{1},

(28l)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax\rho-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\sigma

\displaystyle=\tau\rho-\bar{\tau}\sigma-{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1},

(28m)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1264\relax\lambda-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mu

\displaystyle=\bar{\tau}\mu-\tau\lambda-{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}.

(28n)

The Bianchi Identities take the form

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\Psi_{0}

\displaystyle=-6\varphi_{0}\mathchar 1264\relax\varphi_{0}+(\pi-2\bar{\tau})\Psi_{0}+4\rho{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}

\displaystyle+3\varphi_{0}^{2}(2\tau-\bar{\pi})+6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\rho-6\varphi_{0}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\sigma,

(29a)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\Psi_{0}-\mathchar 1264\relax{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}

\displaystyle=6\varphi_{0}\mathchar 1264\relax\varphi_{1}+(2{\underline{\omega}}-\mu)\Psi_{0}-5\tau{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}+3\sigma{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}

\displaystyle+6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}\sigma-3\varphi_{0}^{2}\bar{\lambda}-3\varphi_{1}^{2}\rho-6\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\sigma-12\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\tau,

(29b)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}

\displaystyle=6\varphi_{1}\mathchar 1264\relax’\varphi_{0}-\lambda\Psi_{0}+(2\pi-\bar{\tau}){\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}+3\rho{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}

\displaystyle+3\varphi_{0}^{2}\mu+6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\pi+3\varphi_{1}^{2}\bar{\sigma}-6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\bar{\tau},

(29c)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}-\mathchar 1264\relax{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-6\varphi_{1}\mathchar 1264\relax’\varphi_{1}+({\underline{\omega}}-2\mu){\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}-3\tau{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+2\sigma{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}

\displaystyle+6\varphi_{2}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\sigma+6\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\tau+3\varphi_{1}^{2}\bar{\tau}-6\varphi_{2}\varphi_{1}\rho,

(29d)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-6\bar{\varphi}_{1}\mathchar 1264\relax\bar{\varphi}_{1}-2\lambda{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}+3\pi{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+2\rho{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}

\displaystyle-3\bar{\varphi}_{1}^{2}\bar{\pi}+6\varphi_{0}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\mu-6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\lambda-6\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\pi,

(29e)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}-\mathchar 1264\relax{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}

\displaystyle=6\bar{\varphi}_{1}\mathchar 1264\relax\varphi_{2}-3\mu{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}-\tau{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}+\sigma\Psi_{4}-3\bar{\varphi}_{1}^{2}\bar{\lambda}-3\varphi_{2}^{2}\bar{\rho},

(29f)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\Psi_{4}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}

\displaystyle=6\varphi_{2}\mathchar 1264\relax’\bar{\varphi}_{1}-3\lambda{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+(4\pi+\bar{\tau}){\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}+\rho\Psi_{4}

\displaystyle+12\varphi_{2}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\pi+6\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\lambda+3\bar{\varphi}_{1}^{2}\mu+3\varphi_{2}^{2}\bar{\sigma}-6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}\lambda,

(29g)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}-\mathchar 1264\relax\Psi_{4}

\displaystyle=-6\varphi_{2}\mathchar 1264\relax’\varphi_{2}-(4\mu+{\underline{\omega}}){\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}+\tau\Psi_{4}

\displaystyle+6\varphi_{2}\varphi_{1}\lambda-6\varphi_{2}\bar{\varphi}_{1}\mu-3\varphi_{2}^{2}\bar{\tau}.

(29h)

Finally, we observe that the commutators are given by

\displaystyle(\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}-\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1278\relax)f

\displaystyle=s({\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}-\bar{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+\pi\tau-\bar{\pi}\bar{\tau})f-{\underline{\omega}}\mathchar 1278\relax f+(\pi+\bar{\tau})\mathchar 1264\relax f+(\bar{\pi}+\tau)\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f,

(30a)

\displaystyle(\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1264\relax-\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1278\relax)f

\displaystyle=s(-\mathchar 1264\relax\rho+\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\sigma+\tau\rho-\bar{\tau}\sigma-\bar{\pi}\rho+\pi\sigma)f+(\bar{\pi}-\tau)\mathchar 1278\relax f+\rho\mathchar 1264\relax f+\sigma\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f

(30b)

\displaystyle(\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax-\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime})f

\displaystyle=s(-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\bar{\lambda}+\mathchar 1264\relax\mu)f-\mu\mathchar 1264\relax f-\bar{\lambda}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f,

(30c)

\displaystyle(\mathchar 1278\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1278\relax)f

\displaystyle=s(\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\rho-\mathchar 1264\relax\bar{\sigma}-\bar{\tau}\rho+\tau\bar{\sigma}+\pi\rho-\bar{\pi}\bar{\sigma})f+(\bar{\tau}-\pi)\mathchar 1278\relax f+\bar{\sigma}\mathchar 1264\relax f+\rho\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f,

(30d)

\displaystyle(\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime})f

\displaystyle=s(\mathchar 1264\relax\lambda-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mu)f-\lambda\mathchar 1264\relax f-\bar{\mu}\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}f,

(30e)

\displaystyle(\mathchar 1264\relax\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}-\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\mathchar 1264\relax)f

\displaystyle=sKf,

(30f)

where
K
is the
Gaussian curvature
of the 2-surfaces

\mathcal{S}
—namely,

\displaystyle K\equiv 2\mu\rho-\lambda\sigma-\bar{\lambda}\bar{\sigma}-{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}-\bar{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+6\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1}.

4
Preliminaries

In this section we provide a first discussion of the general structure
and strategy for the analysis of the formation of trapped surfaces.

4.1
Further ancillary fields

We begin by observing that for Minkowski data
on
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{0}\equiv\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{\star}
the two expansions are
given by

\displaystyle\theta_{{\bm{l}}}(u,v=0)=-\rho-\bar{\rho}=-2\rho=-2\cdot\frac{1}{u-v}=-\frac{2}{u};

\displaystyle\theta_{{\bm{n}}}(u,v=0)=\mu+\bar{\mu}=2\mu=2\cdot\frac{1}{u-v}=\frac{2}{u},

where we have made use of the null coordinates
(u,v)
. For
convenience, we define a new quantity

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}\equiv\mu-\frac{1}{u},

to analyse the behaviour of
\mu
. This renormalized expansion
satisfies transport equations given by

\displaystyle D\tilde{\mu}-\rho\tilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\rho\tilde{\mu}+\mu\rho+{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+\lambda\sigma+\delta\pi+\pi\bar{\pi}-\bar{\alpha}\pi+\beta\pi,

(31a)

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{\mu}+2\mu\tilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mu}^{2}-(\gamma+\bar{\gamma})\mu-\lambda\bar{\lambda}-3\varphi_{2}^{2}.

(31b)

In the T-weight formalism these equations can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax\tilde{\mu}-\mathchar 1264\relax\pi-\rho\tilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\rho\tilde{\mu}+\mu\rho+{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+\lambda\sigma+\pi\bar{\pi},

(32a)

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}\tilde{\mu}+2\mu\tilde{\mu}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mu}^{2}-{\underline{\omega}}\mu-\lambda\bar{\lambda}-3\varphi_{2}^{2}.

(32b)

In view of the subsequent analysis it convenient to define the scalar

\displaystyle{\underline{\chi}}\equiv D\log Q,

which, as it is the derivative of a component of frame, is at the same
level of connection coefficients. This scalar is the direct analogue
of
\chi
as defined in
[
15
]
with a derivative in the
incoming direction. It provides a component of the connection which
does not arise in the original NP formalism, but is needed to obtain a
complete set of outgoing equations for the frame. From the definition
of
{\underline{\chi}}
and the NP Ricci identities we readily obtain

\displaystyle\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime}{\underline{\chi}}=2\tau\pi+2\bar{\tau}\bar{\pi}+2\pi\bar{\pi}+{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+\bar{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2}+6\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}+{\underline{\omega}}{\underline{\chi}}.

(33)

Now, as a consequence of the gauge choice
Q=1

on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
, the initial data for
{\underline{\chi}}
is 0
on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
.

Remark 10
.

From the definitions we have that

\displaystyle s({\tilde{\mu}})=s({\underline{\chi}})=0.

4.2
Signatures and scale-invariant norms

Following the assumptions in
[
28
]
and
[
29
]
, we
assume throughout that on the initial outgoing null
slice
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
, the connection coefficients satisfy
asymptotic behaviour

\displaystyle|\rho|\lesssim\frac{1}{|u_{\infty}|},\quad|\sigma|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|},\quad|\mu|\lesssim\frac{1}{|u_{\infty}|},\quad|{\tilde{\mu}}|=|\mu+\frac{1}{|u_{\infty}|}|\lesssim\frac{1}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}},\quad|\lambda|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}},

\displaystyle|{\underline{\omega}}|\lesssim\frac{a}{|u_{\infty}|^{3}},\quad|\tau|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}},\quad|\pi|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}},

and that for the components of the Weyl tensor one has

\displaystyle|\Psi_{0}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|},\quad|\Psi_{1}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}},\quad|\Psi_{2}|\lesssim\frac{a}{|u_{\infty}|^{3}},\quad|\Psi_{3}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{3}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{4}},\quad|\Psi_{4}|\lesssim\frac{a^{2}}{|u_{\infty}|^{5}}.

For the scalar field
\varphi
and the auxiliary field associated to
its derivative
\varphi_{a}
, we assume that on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}

one has that

\displaystyle|\varphi|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|},\quad|\varphi_{0}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|},\quad|\varphi_{2}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}},\quad|\varphi_{1}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}}.

From the above assumption on
\varphi_{a}
it follows that

\displaystyle|\tilde{\Psi}_{1}|

\displaystyle\equiv|\Psi_{1}-3\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{2}},

\displaystyle|\tilde{\Psi}_{2}|

\displaystyle\equiv|\Psi_{2}-\varphi_{0}\varphi_{2}+\varphi_{1}\bar{\varphi}_{1}|\lesssim\frac{a}{|u_{\infty}|^{3}},

\displaystyle|\tilde{\Psi}_{3}|

\displaystyle\equiv|\Psi_{3}-3\varphi_{2}\bar{\varphi}_{1}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{3}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{4}},

which show that the renormalised Weyl components can be assumed to
keep the same behaviour as that of
\Psi_{1}
,
\Psi_{2}
and
\Psi_{3}
,
respectively. The equivalence between
\Psi
and
{\tilde{\Psi}}
is given in
the main proof.

For future use is convenient to introduce another auxiliary field
—namely,

\displaystyle{\tilde{\varphi}}_{2}\equiv\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\varphi_{2}+\mu\bar{\varphi}_{1},

and assume on
\mathcal{N}_{\star}
that

\displaystyle|{\tilde{\varphi}}_{2}|\lesssim\frac{a^{\frac{3}{2}}}{|u_{\infty}|^{4}}.

Remark 11
.

Note that applying
\mathchar 1264\relax
or
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}
to our variables results
in additional decay of
1/|u|
to the asymptotic decay so
that
\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\varphi_{2}
decays
like
a^{\frac{1}{2}}/|u_{\infty}|^{3}
. The term
\mu\bar{\varphi}_{1}

shares the same decay. The leading (negative) piece of
\mu

eliminates the leading term of these two expressions. A more
detailed discussions on the behaviour of the
quantity
{\tilde{\varphi}}_{2}
is given in Section
7
on
the energy estimates.

In our analysis we follow the basic design in An’s work
[
28
]

for the definition of scale
invariant
L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S})
-norms with
|u|
and
a

weights such that

\displaystyle||\phi||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S})}\lesssim 1.

To this end associate a number (
signature
)
s_{2}(f)
to
characterise the decay rate of the various geometric quantities:

\Psi_{0}

\Psi_{1}

\Psi_{2}

\Psi_{3}

\Psi_{4}

\rho,\sigma

\omega

\vartheta

\tau

\pi

\mu,\lambda,\tilde{\mu}

{\underline{\omega}}

{\underline{\chi}}

\varphi,\varphi_{0}

\varphi_{1}

\varphi_{2}

s_{2}

0

1/2

1

3/2

2

0

0

1/2

1/2

1/2

1

1

0

0

1/2

1

The signature is required to satisfy

\displaystyle s_{2}(\mathchar 1278\relax^{i}\mathchar 1278\relax^{\prime j}\{\mathchar 1264\relax,\mathchar 1264\relax^{\prime}\}^{k}\phi)=s_{2}(\phi)+0\times i+1\times j+\tfrac{1}{2}\times k,

\displaystyle s_{2}(\phi_{1}\cdot\phi_{2})=s_{2}(\phi_{1})+s_{2}(\phi_{2}).

We make use of the signature to define the following

scale-invariant norms
:

\displaystyle||\phi||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\equiv a^{-s_{2}(\phi)}|u|^{2s_{2}(\phi)+1}||\phi||_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle||\phi||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\equiv a^{-s_{2}(\phi)}|u|^{2s_{2}(\phi)}||\phi||_{L^{2}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle||\phi||_{L^{1}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\equiv a^{-s_{2}(\phi)}|u|^{2s_{2}(\phi)-1}||\phi||_{L^{1}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}.

The above norms satisfy the Hölder inequalities

\displaystyle||\phi_{1}\cdot\phi_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|u|}||\phi_{1}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||\phi_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle||\phi_{1}\cdot\phi_{2}||_{L^{1}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|u|}||\phi_{1}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||\phi_{2}||_{L^{1}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle||\phi_{1}\cdot\phi_{2}||_{L^{1}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|u|}||\phi_{1}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||\phi_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}.

Thus it follows that, making use of these norms, that if the norms
themselves are order
1
, then the nonlinear terms can be treated as
lower order terms when
|u|\gg 1
. Finally, we define the following
scale-invariant norms along the lightcones:

\displaystyle||\phi||^{2}_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}_{u}(0,v))}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{0}^{v}||\phi||^{2}_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v^{\prime}})}\mathrm{d}v^{\prime},

\displaystyle||\phi||^{2}_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\frac{a}{|u^{\prime}|^{2}}||\phi||^{2}_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u^{\prime},v})}\mathrm{d}u^{\prime}.

Remark 12
.

In the analysis of main proof, from Section
5
to
Section
7
, we frequently use the Hölder
inequalities to divide the
L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})
of multiple
terms to one
L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})
norm and rest

L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})
norms. Take multiple terms

A\cdot B\cdot C
as an example:

\displaystyle||A\cdot B\cdot C||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\leq\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}||A||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||B||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}||C||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}.

The total number of spherical derivatives in each such term is less or
equal to 11. The reason
A
takes the
L^{2}
norm is that
A
is the
term containing the highest spherical derivative among
A
,
B
and

C
. According to the bootstrap assumptions and the field equations
themselves, no terms which are quadratic (or higher order) in the top
(11th) derivative occur. Therefore, for convenience we use

\displaystyle||A\cdot B\cdot C||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}\leq\frac{1}{|u|^{2}}||A||\times||B||\times||C||

in the discussion when there is no ambiguity.

4.3
Bootstrap norms

In this section we introduce the norms that will be used to set up our
main bootstrap argument. In the following,
let
\hat{\Gamma}\equiv\{\rho,\tau,\pi,{\underline{\omega}},{\underline{\chi}}\}
,

\hat{\Psi}=\{{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1},{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2},{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3},\Psi_{4}\}
. For
0\leq i\leq 6
,
we define

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i,\infty}(u,v)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\sigma||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\hat{\Gamma}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\lambda||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}

\displaystyle+\frac{a}{|u|^{2}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\mu||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+\frac{a}{|u|}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\tilde{\mu}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle\Psi_{i,\infty}(u,v)\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\Psi_{0}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\hat{\Psi}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle{\bm{\varphi}}_{i,\infty}(u,v)\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\varphi_{0}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\varphi_{1},{\tilde{\varphi}}_{2}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\varphi_{2}||_{L^{\infty}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}.

For
0\leq i\leq 10
, we define

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i,2}(u,v)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\sigma||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\hat{\Gamma}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\lambda||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}

\displaystyle+\frac{a}{|u|^{2}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\mu||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+\frac{a}{|u|}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\tilde{\mu}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle{\bm{\varphi}}_{i,2}(u,v)\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\varphi_{0}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\varphi_{1}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u|}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\varphi_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}.

For
0\leq i\leq 9
, we define

\displaystyle\tilde{{\bm{\varphi}}}_{i,2}(u,v)

\displaystyle\equiv||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}{\tilde{\varphi}}_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})},

\displaystyle\Psi_{i,2}(u,v)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\Psi_{0}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\hat{\Psi}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}.

For
0\leq i\leq 10
, we define

\displaystyle\Psi_{i}(u,v)\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\Psi_{0}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}_{u}(0,v))}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\{{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1},{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2},{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3}\}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}_{u}(0,v))},

\displaystyle\underline{\Psi}_{i}(u,v)\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}{\tilde{\Psi}}_{1}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\{{\tilde{\Psi}}_{2},{\tilde{\Psi}}_{3},\Psi_{4}\}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))}.

For
0\leq i\leq 11
, we define

\displaystyle{\bm{\varphi}}_{i}(u,v)\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{0}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}_{u}(0,v))}+||(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{1}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}_{u}(0,v))},

\displaystyle\underline{{\bm{\varphi}}}_{i}(u,v)\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{1}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))}+||\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u^{\prime}|}(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))},

where

\displaystyle||\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u^{\prime}|}(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{2}||^{2}_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))}\equiv\int_{u_{\infty}}^{u}\frac{a^{2}}{|u^{\prime}|^{4}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{2}||^{2}_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u^{\prime},v})}.

Finally, for
0\leq i\leq 10
, we define

\displaystyle\underline{\tilde{{\bm{\varphi}}}}_{i}(u,v)\equiv||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}{\tilde{\varphi}}_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))}.

Remark 13
.

From the choice of signature for
{\tilde{\Psi}}
and
\Psi
, it
follows that the norm of
{\tilde{\Psi}}
is equivalent to that of
\Psi
.

In the following we denote by
\Gamma_{i,\infty}
,
\Gamma_{i,2}
,

\Psi_{i,\infty}
,
\Psi_{i,2}
,
\Psi_{i}
,
\underline{\Psi}_{i}
,

{\bm{\varphi}}_{i,\infty}
,
{\bm{\varphi}}_{i,2}
,
{\bm{\varphi}}_{i}
and

\underline{{\bm{\varphi}}}_{i}
the supremum over
u
and
v
of the
aforementioned bootstrap norms. We define, moreover

\displaystyle{\bm{\Gamma}}\equiv\sum_{i\leq 6}(\Gamma_{i,\infty}+\Psi_{i,\infty}+{\bm{\varphi}}_{i,\infty})+\sum_{i\leq 10}(\Gamma_{i,2}+{\bm{\varphi}}_{i,2})+\sum_{i\leq 9}(\Psi_{i,2}+\tilde{{\bm{\varphi}}}_{i,2}),

\displaystyle\bm{\Psi}\equiv\sum_{i\leq 10}(\Psi_{i}+\underline{\Psi}_{i}),\quad{\bm{\varphi}}\equiv\sum_{i\leq 11}({\bm{\varphi}}_{i}+\underline{{\bm{\varphi}}}_{i})+\sum_{i\leq 10}\underline{\tilde{{\bm{\varphi}}}}_{i}.

and write
{\bm{\Gamma}}_{0}
,
\bm{\Psi}_{0}
and
{\bm{\varphi}}_{0}
for the norms on
the initial hypersurfaces.

We make use of
\Gamma(\Gamma,\varphi_{i},\Psi)_{k}
to
denote the k-th derivative of
L^{2}
norm of
\Gamma
,
\varphi_{i}
and
\Psi

which shows up on the right hand side in previous definition.
Take
\sigma
as an example:

\displaystyle\Gamma(\sigma)_{k}\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{D})^{i}\sigma||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{S}_{u,v})}

Follow the same spirit we make use of
\bm{\Gamma}[\Gamma]
to denote
all the norms related to
\Gamma
. For
\sigma
we define

\displaystyle\bm{\Gamma}(\sigma)\equiv\sum_{i\leq 10}\Gamma(\sigma)_{i}

Moreover, for
\varphi_{i}
, we use
\bm{\varphi}[\varphi_{i}]_{k}

and
\underline{\bm{\varphi}}[\varphi_{i}]_{k}
to denote the norm of
the
k

\varphi_{i}
along null
hypersurfaces
\mathcal{N}_{u}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}
respectively.
For example:

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}[\varphi_{0}]_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}||(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{0}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}_{u}(0,v))},

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{\varphi}}[\varphi_{2}]_{k}

\displaystyle\equiv||\frac{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}{|u^{\prime}|}(a^{\frac{1}{2}})^{i-1}\mathcal{D}^{i}\varphi_{2}||_{L^{2}_{sc}(\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{v}(u_{\infty},u))}.

We then write
\bm{\varphi}[\varphi_{i}]
to denote the sum
of
\bm{\varphi}[\varphi_{i}]_{k}
up to order
11. By
